# ROUND 2/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Score is MINIMISED (lower = better)
- Delivering items from one half DECREASES score by 1 (good)
- Delivering items from the other half INCREASES score by 1 (bad)
- Need to identify: which color wall (BLUE or GREEN) corresponds to the "good" items

**Champion analysis:**
- `--s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 5` = GoToColor targeting color 5 (magenta). Score: -2.00
- This single-state machine goes toward magenta objects and achieves -2.00
- Color codes: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan
- The wall colors are RED (nest side), BLUE (one source half), GREEN (other source half)
- Colors 2=green and 3=blue correspond to the source halves

Wait - the champion targets color 5 (magenta) which is the LED color of other robots, not walls. This means robots are attracted to each other while exploring, which helps them find the source. But with no way to distinguish good from bad items...

Actually, GoToColor toward magenta (color 5) means going toward magenta-lit robots. This creates a swarm that aggregates. Score -2.00 might just be from random foraging.

**Strategy:** I need robots to:
1. Go to the "good" source (one of blue/green walls)
2. Return to nest (white floor, red wall)
3. Avoid the "bad" source

Key perceptions: floor color (black=source, white=nest, gray=transit), wall colors (blue/green for sources, red for nest).

Let me design FSMs that:
- Exploit the champion (GoToColor magenta)
- Try GoToColor toward blue (3) or green (2) walls specifically
- Use floor transitions: black→go to white, white→go to black

**Machines to generate:**

1. **Survival**: Exact copy of champion
2. **Exploitation**: Variants of champion with different parameters
3. **Exploration**: Multi-state foraging machines

For multi-state: State 0 = explore/go to source (black floor), when on black go toward nest (white/red). Try targeting color 2 (green walls) vs color 3 (blue walls) to find which is "good."

Let me design 14 machines:

FSM: --nstates 1 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --cle0 6 --clr0 5

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --cle0 5 --clr0 3 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 0.9 --s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --cle1 6 --clr1 1 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 0.9

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 2:**
The champion is:
- State 0: GoToColor **blue** (clr=3), on BLACK floor → go to state 1 (p=0.9)
- State 1: GoToColor **red** (clr=1), on WHITE floor → go to state 0 (p=0.9)

This achieves -12.44! So:
- **BLUE** wall = good source (items that DECREASE score)
- **GREEN** wall = bad source (items that INCREASE score)
- State 0: Go toward blue wall (find good items), when on black floor (source area), transition to return state
- State 1: Go toward red wall (return to nest), when on white floor (nest), transition to collect again

The worst performer was GoToColor **green** (clr=2) → score +11.51, confirming green is the bad source.

**Exploitation strategy:**
1. Keep champion exact
2. Vary transition probabilities (0.9 → try 0.95, 0.85, 1.0)
3. Add more transitions (e.g., also transition on gray floor detection, or add neighbor-based transitions)
4. Try 3-state variants: explore → go to blue → return to nest
5. Try adding a state that avoids green wall

**Critical insight:** The champion works by:
- GoToColor blue (approach good source)
- When on black floor (picked up item), switch to GoToColor red (go to nest)
- When on white floor (delivered), switch back

Let me design exploitations and explorations:

- Vary p from 0.9 to 0.95 or 0.85
- Add a second transition (gray floor) to make return more robust
- Try 3-state: GoToBlue → GoToRed → (explore buffer)
- Try GoAwayColor green + GoToColor blue combination
- Try adding neighbor conditions

## Reasoning

**Understanding the champion (score -13.78):**
- State 0: GoToColor blue (clr=3) → when on BLACK floor (p=0.9) → go to state 1
- State 1: GoToColor red (clr=1) → when on WHITE floor (p=0.9) → go to state 0; when on BLACK floor (p=0.3) → go to state 1 (self-loop... wait, can't self-loop)

Wait, state 1 has transitions: n1x0 goes to state 0 (destination index 0 among others = state 0), n1x1 goes to state 1 (destination index 1 among others = state 2). So:
- State 1: GoToColor red, on WHITE → state 0; on BLACK → state 2
- State 2: GoAwayColor green (clr=2), on WHITE (p=0.8) → state 1; on BLACK (p=0.7) → state 0

So state 2 is a "recovery" state that moves away from green (bad source) when the robot accidentally ends up there.

**Key insights:**
1. The 3-state machine beats the 2-state ones significantly (-13.78 vs -12.85)
2. State 2 (GoAwayColor green) acts as avoidance of the bad source
3. The recovery mechanism (avoiding green/bad source) adds value

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Vary probabilities slightly in champion
- Try different recovery behaviors (explore instead of GoAwayColor)
- Try adding a 4th state

**Exploration:**
- Try: GoToBlue → GoToRed → GoToBlue again (double confirmation)
- Try: Swap GoAwayColor green for GoToColor blue in recovery
- Try: Add exploration state between transitions
- Try: Use floor=gray as additional trigger
- Try: Modify GoAwayColor green parameters

Let me note: with 3 states, destination indices: from state 0 (others are [1,2]), from state 1 (others are [0,2]), from state 2 (others are [0,1]).

Champion state 1: n1x0 destination=0 → state 0, n1x1 destination=1 → state 2. ✓
Champion state 2: n2x0 destination=1 → state 1, n2x1 destination=0 → state 0. ✓

## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (score -14.98):**
- State 0: GoToColor blue (clr=3), on BLACK (p=0.9) → state 1
- State 1: GoToColor red (clr=1), on WHITE (p=0.9) → state 2; on BLACK (p=0.3) → state 2
- State 2: GoToColor blue (clr=3), on BLACK (p=0.85) → state 1; on WHITE (p=0.9) → state 0
- State 3: GoAwayColor green (clr=2), on WHITE (p=0.8) → state 2; on BLACK (p=0.7) → state 0

Wait, let me re-read the champion carefully:
- 4 states: s0, s1, s2, s3
- s0: GoToColor blue → on BLACK → go to s1
- s1: GoToColor red → on WHITE → s2; on BLACK → s2 (both go to s2!)
- s2: GoToColor blue → on BLACK → s1; on WHITE → s0
- s3: GoAwayColor green → on WHITE → s2; on BLACK → s0

But s3 is never reached from s0, s1, or s2! s0 only goes to s1, s1 only goes to s2, s2 goes to s1 or s0. State 3 is unreachable. Yet this scored -14.98.

Actually wait - the destination encoding: with 4 states, from state 1, "others" are [0, 2, 3]. So n1x0 destination=2 means state 2, n1x1 destination=2 means state 2 (same!). Both transitions of s1 go to state 2. From state 2, n2x0 destination=1 means state 1, n2x1 destination=0 means state 0. State 3 is indeed unreachable.

So effectively this is a 3-state machine where:
- s0: GoToBlue, black→s1
- s1: GoToRed, white→s2, black→s2 (always goes to s2 quickly)
- s2: GoToBlue, black→s1, white→s0

The loop s0→s1→s2→s1 keeps robots doing: go to blue, go to red, go to blue, go to red... with s0 as a "reset" state.

**Key pattern:** GoToBlue (collect) → GoToRed (deliver) cycle with floor-color triggers.

**Exploitation ideas:**
1. Fine-tune probabilities in champion
2. Make state 3 reachable (add GoAwayGreen as actual avoidance)
3. Try higher probabilities for transitions
4. Try adding gray floor trigger to help navigation

**For 4-state with reachable s3:**
- s0: GoToBlue, black→s1
- s1: GoToRed, white→s2, black→s3 (if still on black after collecting, avoid green)
- s2: GoToBlue, black→s1, white→s0
- s3: GoAwayGreen, white→s1, black→s2

Let me also try variations of probabilities and the 3-state champion topology.

**Champion analysis (score -15.68):**
The champion is a 4-state machine where state 3 has 3 transitions (including going to state 1 when on gray floor p=0.3). Let me parse carefully:

- s0: GoToColor blue, BLACK(p=0.9)→s1
- s1: GoToColor red, WHITE(p=0.9)→s2, BLACK(p=0.3)→s2 (both go to s2)
- s2: GoToColor blue, BLACK(p=0.8)→s1, WHITE(p=0.85)→s0
- s3: GoAwayColor green, WHITE(p=0.8)→s2, BLACK(p=0.7)→s0, GRAY(p=0.3)→s1

State 3 is still unreachable (s0→s1, s1→s2, s2→s0 or s1). But it still scores best!

The core loop: GoToBlue → GoToRed (when on black) → GoToBlue (cycling between s0 and s2 via s1). The robots:
1. Go toward blue wall to reach source
2. When on black floor (source), switch to GoToRed (heading to nest)
3. When on white floor (nest), switch back to GoToBlue
4. When on black again from s2, switch back to GoToRed via s1

**Key observations:**
- The 4-state topology with unreachable s3 still beats 3-state — perhaps the optimizer found good parameters for the s0→s1→s2 loop
- State 2 going to s0 on WHITE (p=0.85) and s1 on BLACK (p=0.8) is the key mechanism
- The s2 BLACK transition (p=0.8) going back to s1 (GoToRed) prevents robots from staying in source

**Exploitation:**
1. Vary p2x0 (0.8), p2x1 (0.85) slightly
2. Vary p1x1 (0.3) - the black floor transition from s1
3. Try making s3 reachable from s0 or s2
4. Try adding gray floor trigger to s0 or s2
5. Combine best features: 3 transitions in s3, reachable

**Making s3 reachable:** From s2, add a transition to s3 when perceiving green (color 2). This would make robots actively avoid green source.

## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (score -18.07):**
- s0: GoToColor blue (clr=3), BLACK(p=0.9)→s1
- s1: GoToColor red (clr=1), WHITE(p=0.9)→s2, BLACK(p=0.3)→s2, perceives green/color2(p=0.7)→s2 [all go to s2!]
- s2: GoToColor blue (clr=3), BLACK(p=0.8)→s1, WHITE(p=0.85)→s0
- s3: GoAwayColor green (unreachable)

The key innovation: s1 now has 3 transitions, all going to s2. The third transition is "perceives green (color 2) with p=0.7 → s2". This means when the robot is in delivery mode (GoToRed) and sees the green source, it switches back to GoToBlue. This avoids accidentally collecting from the bad source.

**Insight:** The green-perception trigger in s1 is what drives the improvement from -15.68 to -18.07. When heading to nest and seeing green, switch to go-to-blue mode to avoid the green source.

**Exploitation strategy:**
1. Keep champion exact
2. Vary p1x2 (green perception probability): try 0.6, 0.8, 0.5
3. Vary p0x0 (black floor in s0): try 0.85, 0.95
4. Add similar green-avoidance trigger to s0 or s2
5. Make s3 reachable by connecting from s1 when perceiving green
6. Try adding gray floor trigger to s2

**Key: The 3 transitions in s1 all go to s2.** Let me try making the green-perception go to s3 (GoAwayGreen) instead of s2, making s3 reachable and useful.

From s1 with 4 states: others are [0,2,3]. n1x2 destination=2 means state 3. Let me try that.

Also try: in s2, add a perception of green → s3 (GoAwayGreen) to make s3 active.

**Destination encoding reminder (4 states):**
- From s0: others=[1,2,3], D=0→s1, D=1→s2, D=2→s3
- From s1: others=[0,2,3], D=0→s0, D=1→s2, D=2→s3
- From s2: others=[0,1,3], D=0→s0, D=1→s1, D=2→s3
- From s3: others=[0,1,2], D=0→s0, D=1→s1, D=2→s2

Champion s1: n1x0 D=2→s2✓, n1x1 D=2→s2✓, n1x2 D=2→s2✓
Champion s2: n2x0 D=1→s1✓, n2x1 D=0→s0✓
Champion s3: n3x0 D=2→s2✓, n3x1 D=0→s0✓, n3x2 D=1→s1✓ (unreachable)

## Reasoning

**New champion: -18.14** (barely improved from -18.07). The new champion differs from the previous in:
- s2: p2x0=0.85 (was 0.8), p2x1=0.9 (was 0.85) — slightly higher probabilities
- s3: now has 4 transitions with n3x3 = perceive blue (color 3) → s1, p=0.55

So s3 (GoAwayGreen, unreachable) has gained a 4th transition: perceive blue → s1. Still unreachable but the parameters matter.

**Key findings:**
- p3x3 = 0.55 (blue perception in s3) beats 0.6 and 0.7
- s2 with p2x0=0.85, p2x1=0.9 slightly better than 0.8/0.85
- Adding green-perception to s2 (3 transitions) hurts
- The topology is very stable: all top performers share the same structure

**What to try in round 9:**
1. Survival: exact champion
2. Exploit: vary p3x3 (0.55→0.45, 0.5) in s3
3. Exploit: combine 4-transition s3 with s2 params from champion (0.85/0.9)
4. Exploit: vary p3x0/p3x1/p3x2 in champion
5. Exploit: vary p1x1 (0.3→0.2, 0.25) with champion's s2
6. Exploit: try p2x0=0.9, p2x1=0.9 (even higher)
7. Explore: make s3 reachable via s0 or s2
8. Explore: add transition to s0 that avoids green

**Note on making s3 reachable:**
From s2 (4-state machine), others=[0,1,3]:
- D=0→s0, D=1→s1, D=2→s3

If s2's WHITE transition goes to s3 instead of s0 (D=2), s3 becomes reachable after delivery. Then s3 (GoAwayGreen) helps avoid picking up bad items before returning to s0 (GoToBlue).

Let me try: s2: black→s1 (D=1), white→s3 (D=2); s3: white→s0 (D=0→s0), black→s1 (D=1→s1), gray→s1 (D=1→s1), blue-perception→s1(D=1→s1)

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 10 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.49 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
